The Functional Machine Calculus II: Semantics
Chris Barrett, Willem Heijltjes, Guy McCusker

Abstract
The Functional Machine Calculus (FMC), recently introduced by the second author, is a generalization of the lambda-calculus which may faithfully encode the effects of higher-order mutable store, I/O and probabilistic/non-deterministic input. Significantly, it remains confluent and can be simply typed in the presence of these effects. In this paper, we explore the denotational semantics of the FMC. We have three main contributions: first, we argue that its syntax – in which both effects and lambda-calculus are realised using the same syntactic constructs – is semantically natural, corresponding closely to the structure of a Scott-style domain theoretic semantics. Second, we show that simple types confer strong normalization by extending Gandy’s proof for the lambda-calculus, including a small simplification of the technique. Finally, we show that the typed FMC (without considering the specifics of encoded effects), modulo an appropriate equational theory, is a complete language for Cartesian closed categories.

Typed lambda-calculi with sharing and unsharing

1
Introduction

Almost without exception, modern programming languages support a combination of computational effects and higher-order mechanisms. Programmers and programming language theorists recognise that the effects and higher-order mechanisms are fundamentally different constructs, with radically different syntax and semantics: compare assignment and dereferencing operations with function definition and invocation, for example. In both operational and denotational accounts, the higher-order mechanism — typically expressed in some variant of
\lambda
-calculus — and the effects mechanisms are treated using distinct approaches, and indeed the combination of the two, not to mention the combination of multiple kinds of effects, requires careful handling.

In a previous paper
[
6
]
the second author introduced the Functional Machine Calculus (FMC), a compact programming language which eliminates these distinctions and supports higher-order effectful programming with a streamlined yet natural syntax and operational semantics. In this paper, we reprise the definition of the FMC and attempt to explain and explore its construction and behaviour from a denotational perspective. Beginning with a domain-theoretic analysis of computation with stacks, we discover that the
\lambda
-calculus and effectful aspects of programming can be viewed as being of exactly the same kind, in fact entirely interchangeable. The syntax and operational semantics of the FMC embodies the operations naturally supported by the denotational semantics, while remaining programmer-friendly: the prevous paper
[
6
]
demonstrates by example the wide range of effectful programs that can straightforwardly be expressed in this syntax.

The connection we exploit between domain-theoretic semantics of
\lambda
-calculus and an operational semantics based on stacks is not new. In
[
19
]
, Streicher and Reus show that Scott’s well-known
D_{\infty}
models motivate and explain the definition of Krivine’s abstract machine for evaluating
\lambda
-terms. Scott’s construction takes a domain
D
and builds a model of the
\lambda
-calculus as an appropriate limit of a sequence of domains, shown on the left below. Streicher and Reus observed that this construction may alternatively be regarded as taking the limit of the sequence on the right, where each
D_{n}
is recovered as
C_{n}\rightarrow D
, and
D_{\infty}
is
C_{\infty}\rightarrow D
.

\begin{array}[t]{rcl}D_{0}&=&D\\
D_{n+1}&=&D_{n}\rightarrow D_{n}\end{array}\quad\quad\quad\quad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}C_{0}&=&1\\
C_{n+1}&=&C_{n}\times(C_{n}\rightarrow D)\end{array}

With this view, the limit
C_{\infty}
is a stream or (potentially infinite) stack of elements of
D_{\infty}
, i.e. denotations of
\lambda
-terms; and such terms consume a stack. The equations defining the interpretations of terms in this model show that the operations of application and abstraction correspond directly to pushing and popping from the stack. The return domain
D
plays a very minor role in the semantics of
\lambda
-terms: in Streicher and Reus’s view, it is the result type of continuations, and ordinary
\lambda
-terms never return. Our development in this paper adapts this in two ways. First, we choose the return domain
D
to be the domain of stacks. Thus a term can be regarded as a
stack transformer
, which immediately supports an operation of sequencing between terms, leading to a sublanguage of the FMC called the
sequential
\lambda
-calculus
. Second, we enrich the domain equation with the familiar monad for global state, and observe that — if states are also stacks — the resulting domain equation is one of multiple-stack transformers, with
no special status
afforded to the stack that implements
\lambda
-abstraction and application. Thus we arrive at the promised semantic explanation of the FMC, treating reader–writer style effects and higher-order mechanisms identically, and giving a denotational motivation for the stack-machine semantics.

We go on to study this calculus from a type-theoretic and category-theoretic perspective. The domain-theoretic model is untyped but, generalising the usual development for
\lambda
-calculus, a simple type system can be imposed which describes the shapes of the stacks being operated upon. Adapting Gandy’s proof technique for
\lambda
-calculus, we show that the well-typed terms of the FMC are strongly normalizing. Further, we study the categorical properties of the calculus, viewing terms as morphisms between types, and discover that, up to a notion of contextual equivalence, well-typed programs form a Cartesian closed category. An equational theory is presented which refines this equivalence, and well-typed terms modulo this theory are shown to be a sound and complete language for CCCs.

The properties identified above — strong normalization, Cartesian closure, and the operational property of confluence established in
[
6
]
—
are entirely expected of languages which are variants of typed
\lambda
-calculus, but perhaps surprising, or even shocking, in the setting of the effectful FMC. How can the combination of higher-order programming and effects, including state, input/output, nondeterminism and probability, retain properties of confluence and referential transparency?

We offer the following explanation. These properties are of the FMC as a general calculus, which remains close to the
\lambda
-calculus, independent of the encoding of effects. As explored in
[
6
]
, the FMC is confluent, and for encoded reader/writer effects this manifests as reduction following the algebraic laws of Plotkin and Power
[
13
]
. The CCC semantics presented in this paper pertains to the FMC itself, and, we emphasize, is
not
a semantics of effects: when particular properties of the encoded effects are taken into account, the semantics will no longer be a CCC. We explore this in more detail in Section
6
.

2
The Functional Machine Calculus

The Functional Machine Calculus (FMC) arises from an operational perspective on the
\lambda
-calculus, taking a simple call–by–name stack machine in the style of Krivine
[
7
]
as primary. The machine evaluates a term in the context of a stack, where application is a
push
(of the argument) and abstraction is a
pop
, binding the popped value to the abstracted variable. Since the stack machine is intended as an operational semantics, and not for implementation, for simplicity we use substitution rather than an environment. The FMC then introduces two natural generalizations.

Locations

The machine is generalized from one to multiple stacks or streams, indexed by a global set of
locations

A
. In the calculus, abstraction and application are parameterized in
A
as pop- and push-actions on the associated stack. Stacks are homogeneous, but may be used to encode different reader/writer effects: an input stream (which may be non-deterministic or probabilistic), an output stream, or a global mutable variable.

Sequencing

The calculus is extended with sequential composition of terms, which gives their consecutive execution on the machine, and an identity term as the unit to composition, which is the empty instruction sequence on the machine, analogous to imperative
skip
. This generalizes the calculus from one of stack
consumers
to stack
transformers
, where a term may return multiple outputs to the stack, and gives control over execution, as demonstrated by the encoding of various reduction strategies including call–by–value and call–by–push–value (see
[
6
]
).

Locations are an innovation of the FMC
[
6
]
, while sequencing is familiar from Hasegawa’s
\kappa
-calculus
[
5
,
16
]
and higher-order stack programming (see e.g.
[
10
]
). In the latter case, there are also certain similarities with Milner’s action calculi
[
9
]
. These two innovations are implemented technically as follows. To emphasize the operational intuition as
push
and
pop
, application
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
will be written as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N].\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and abstraction
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\lambda x.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. These are subsequently parameterized in
locations

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}b\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in A
.
Sequencing
introduces a
nil
or
skip
term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and makes the variable construct a prefix
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
; sequential composition
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\,{;}\,N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
will be a defined operation.

Definition 1
.

The
Functional Machine Calculus
(
FMC
) is given by the grammar

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\Coloneqq\quad{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N]a.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

where from left to right the
term
constructors are
nil
, a
(sequential) variable
, an
application
or
push action
on the location
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and an
abstraction
or
pop action
on the location
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
which binds
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Terms are considered modulo
\alpha
-equivalence.

We may omit the trailing
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
from terms for readability. We will use a
main
location
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\lambda\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, omitted from the term notation, as the computation stack (as opposed to the stacks to interpret effects), on which (call–by–name)
\lambda
-terms embed. We will use constants as free variables; constant operators such as addition
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and conditionals
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{if}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
will operate on
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\lambda\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as well.

Example 2
.

Consider the following example terms.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{rnd}\langle x\rangle.\,[x].\,c\langle y\rangle.\,[y].\,+.\,\langle z\rangle.\,[z]c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\qquad\qquad{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[\langle x\rangle.\,[x]\mathsf{out}.\,[x].\,[1].\,+].\,\langle f\rangle.\,[0].\,f.\,f.\,f\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

The first term increments a memory cell
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with a random number. It pops
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
from the random generator stream
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{rnd}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and pushes it to the main stack; pops
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
from the cell
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and pushes that to the main stack as well; then
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
adds the top two elements of the main stack
x
and
y
pushing back the result
x+y
; and this is popped as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}z\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and pushed back onto the cell
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

The second term counts up from zero to three, outputting
0,1,2
and leaving
3
on the main stack. The subterm
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle.\,[x]\mathsf{out}.\,[x].\,[1].\,+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
pops
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
from the main stack and sends it to the output location
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{out}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and then
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x].\,[1].\,+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
leaves
x+1
on the main stack. In the example, this term is pushed, popped as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}f\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and called three times on zero.

Definition 3
.

Composition

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
substitution

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{N/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are given by

\begin{array}[]{rcll@{\qquad}rcll}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[P]a.\,N\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[P]a.\,(N\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x.\,N\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x.\,(N\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle.\,N\,{;}\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle.\,(N\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\qquad~({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}))\\
\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{P/x\}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{P/x\}[N]a.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[\{P/x\}N]a.\,\{P/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{P/x\}x.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}P\,{;}\,\{P/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{P/x\}a\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{P/x\}y.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}y.\,\{P/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{P/x\}a\langle y\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&=&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle y\rangle.\,\{P/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\qquad({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}))\end{array}

where, in the last two cases,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\neq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
; both are capture-avoiding by the conditions
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}P\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
, which can be satisfied by
\alpha
-conversion.

Lemma 4
.

Composition is associative and has unit
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

Definition 5
.

The
functional abstract machine
is given by the following data. A
stack
of terms
S
is defined below left, and written with the top element to the right. A
memory

S_{A}
is a family of stacks or streams in
A
, defined below right.

S~\Coloneqq~\varepsilon~\mid~S{\cdot}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\qquad S_{A}~\Coloneqq~\{\,S_{a}\,\mid\,a\in A\}

The notation
S_{A};S_{a}
identifies the stack
S_{a}
within
S_{A}
. A
state
is a pair
(S_{A},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
of a memory and a term. The
transitions
or
steps
of the machine are given below left as top–to–bottom rules. A
run
of the machine is a sequence of steps, written as
(S_{A},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\kern 1.0pt{\Downarrow}\kern 1.0pt(T_{A},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
or with a double line as below right.

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}~;~S_{a}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N]a.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\hline\cr S_{A}~;~S_{a}{\cdot}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}~;~S_{a}{\cdot}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\hline\cr S_{A}~;~S_{a}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{N/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}\qquad\qquad\qquad\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\rule[-5.0pt]{0.0pt}{15.0pt}T_{A}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}

Constant operations such as addition
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and conditional
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{if}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
pop the required number of items from the main stack and reinstate their result, as per the rule given below left.
The FMC then operates as a standard stack calculus: e.g. an arithmetic expression
1+((2+3)\times 4)
is given as a term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[4].\,[3].\,[2].\,+.\,\times.\,[1].\,+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
which indeed returns
21
. Formally, a constant operator
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c^{n,m}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
of arity
n,m
is defined by a (partial) function
c^{n,m}
from
n
input terms to
m
output terms, which generates the machine rule schema below right, where the output terms are put on the stack.

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A};S_{\lambda}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}3\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}+.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\hline\cr S_{A};S_{\lambda}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}5\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~,~}r@{~)}}S_{A};S_{\lambda}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\cdots{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c^{n,m}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\hline\cr S_{A};S_{\lambda}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c^{n,m}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(N_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0},\dots,N_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}\qquad\qquad

Example 6
.

The first term of Example
2
has the following run of the machine, where the
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{rnd}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
location is initialized with a stream with
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}3\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
at the head, and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with the value
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}5\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

\begin{array}[]{@{(~}l@{~;~}l@{~;~}l@{~~,~~}r@{~)}}S_{\mathsf{rnd}}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}3\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon_{c}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}5\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon_{\lambda}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{rnd}\langle x\rangle.\,[x].\,c\langle y\rangle.\,[y].\,+.\,\langle z\rangle.\,[z]c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\hline\cr S_{\mathsf{rnd}}&\varepsilon_{c}\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}5\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}&\varepsilon_{\lambda}&{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[3].\,c\langle y\rangle.\,[y].\,+.\,\langle z\rangle.\,[z]c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\

Beta-reduction in the
\lambda
-calculus, from the perspective of the machine, lets consecutive push and pop actions interact directly. With multiple stacks, these must be actions on the same location, while other locations may be accessed in-between. Informally, the reduction step will then be as follows, where the argument
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N]a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and abstraction
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
may be separated by actions
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[P]b\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}b\langle y\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\neq{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}b\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
:
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N]a\dots a\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mathrel{\hbox to10.5pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{
{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}
{}
{}
{}
\lxSVG@stroke}}
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.2 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.5pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.07 0 L 0.35 2.4 L 0 0 L 0.35 -2.4 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\beta}~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\dots\{N/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. In addition, it must be avoided that any intervening
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}b\langle y\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
captures
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}y\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

Definition 7
.

A
head context

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is a sequence of applications and abstractions terminating in a hole:

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\Coloneqq~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[M]a.\,H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle.\,H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

The term denoted
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is given by replacing the hole
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\{\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, where a binder
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
captures in
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. The
binding variables

\mathsf{bv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
of
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are those variables
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is constructed over
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. The set of
locations
used in a term or context is denoted
\mathsf{loc}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
respectively
\mathsf{loc}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
. Then
beta-reduction
and
eta-reduction
are defined respectively by the rewrite rule schemas below, where
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{loc}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
for both reduction rules,
\mathsf{bv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\cap\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})=\varnothing
for the
\beta
-rule, and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\notin\mathsf{bv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
for the
\eta
-rule. Both reductions are closed under all contexts.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N]a.\,H.\,a\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mathrel{\hbox to10.5pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.2 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.5pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.07 0 L 0.35 2.4 L 0 0 L 0.35 -2.4 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\beta}~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H.\,\{N/x\}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\qquad\qquad{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle.\,H.\,[x]a.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mathrel{\hbox to10.5pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{
}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 10.2 0}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.5pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.07 0 L 0.35 2.4 L 0 0 L 0.35 -2.4 Z}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}_{\eta}~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}H.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad(x\notin\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}))

We now clarify the relationship between beta reduction and the machine.
Evaluation of a term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
on the machine, given sufficient inputs in the form of a stack of terms
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\cdots{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, begins in the state
({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\cdots{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
. The machine then implements a particular (weak) reduction strategy, with each
pop
transition corresponding to a beta-reduction of the term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N_{1}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\ldots[N_{n}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}].\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
corresponding to the machine state under evaluation.

In Section
6
, we further introduce a notion of observational equivalence based on the machine, dubbed
machine equivalence
, where terms are considered equivalent if they send equivalent inputs to equivalent outputs. This is shown to validate the beta and eta equations in general.

The two generalizations
locations
and
sequencing
are independent, and the two calculi that have one but not the other are of independent interest.

•

The
poly
\lambda
-calculus
has only
locations
, and is given by the fragment below.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\Coloneqq~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x.\,\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N]a.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}a\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

•

The
sequential
\lambda
-calculus
has only
sequencing
, and is given by the fragment below.

{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\Coloneqq~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N].\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}~\mid~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

Example 8
.

To demonstrate how the FMC encodes both effects and evaluation strategies, we consider the following (standard) call–by–value
\lambda
-calculus with reader/writer effects. (We assume familiarity with the operational semantics of effects and call–by–value
\lambda
-calculus; for an introduction see Winskel
[
20
]
.)

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l@{\qquad}l}M,N,P&\Coloneqq&x~\mid~M\,N~\mid~\lambda x.M&\emph{$\lambda$-calculus}\\[0.0pt]
&\mid&\mathsf{read}~\mid~\mathsf{write}~N;M&\emph{input/output}\\[0.0pt]
&\mid&c:=N;M~\mid~!c&\emph{stateupdateandlookup}\\[0.0pt]
&\mid&N\oplus M~\mid~N+M&\emph{probabilisticandnon-deterministicsum}\end{array}

The full calculus is encoded into the FMC as follows. We let
A
comprise the main location
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\lambda\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, a location
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{in}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for input,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{out}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for output,
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{rnd}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{nd}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
for probabilistically and non-deterministically generated streams of boolean values
(\top,\bot)
, and one location for each global memory cell. A term
M
encodes as
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M_{{v}}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
below, where
N+M
encodes like
N\oplus M
but with the stream
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{nd}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
.

\begin{aligned} \vphantom{(}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}_{v}&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\vphantom{(}(\lambda x.\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}_{v}&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[\langle x\rangle.\,M_{{v}}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\vphantom{(}(M\,N)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}_{v}&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{{v}}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,M_{{v}}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\,{;}\,\langle x\rangle.\,x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{aligned}\quad\!\begin{aligned} \vphantom{(}\mathsf{read}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}_{v}&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{in}\langle x\rangle.\,[x]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\vphantom{(}!c\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}_{v}&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}c\langle x\rangle.\,[x]c.\,[c]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{aligned}\quad\begin{aligned} \vphantom{(}(\mathsf{write}~N\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}_{v}&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{{v}}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\langle x\rangle.\,[x]\mathsf{out}.\,M_{{v}}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\vphantom{(}(c\mathbin{{:}{=}}N\,{;}\,M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}_{v}&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N_{{v}}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,\langle x\rangle.\,c\langle\_\rangle.\,[x]c.\,M_{{v}}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\vphantom{(}(N\oplus M)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}_{v}&={\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\mathsf{rnd}\langle x\rangle.\,[N].\,[M].\,[x].\,\mathsf{if}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{aligned}

We leave it to the reader to confirm that the interpretation generates the correct evaluation behaviour, and conclude the example with the encoding and reduction of the following term.

\begin{aligned} ({(\lambda f.f(f\,0))\,(\lambda x.\mathsf{write}~x;!c)})_{v}&~=~{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[\langle x\rangle.\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\underline{{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[x].\,\langle v\rangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}.\,[v]\mathsf{out}.\,c\langle y\rangle.\,[y]c.\,[y]].\,\langle f\rangle.\,[0].\,[f].\,\langle z\rangle.\,z.\,[f].\,\langle w\rangle.\,w\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
&~\mathrel{\hbox to10.5pt{\vbox to4.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25pt\lower-2.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

3
Domain-theoretic semantics

Our aim in this section is to show that the syntax and stack-machine semantics of the FMC may be further justified by consideration of a simple domain-theoretic semantics.
We work in the category
\mathbf{Cppo}
of complete partial orders (with least-element) and continuous functions. We show that a domain equation for stack-transformers directly supports the operations of the sequential
\lambda
-calculus, directly extending a Scott-style semantics of
\lambda
-calculus. The step from sequential lambda-calculus to FMC is then nothing more than the incorporation of the state monad in the original domain equation. Our development has much in common with Streicher and Reus’s work
[
19
]
; the key step in the move to the FMC is to allow computations to return a result — a new stack — which may be further operated upon by later computations, yielding the sequencing operation of the FMC.

We begin by constructing a domain
D
to interpret terms. A stack can be seen as an element of
D^{\mathbb{N}}
. A term takes a stack and, after computation, returns a new stack as its result. We suppose that computations are modelled using an (unspecified) strong monad
T
on
\mathbf{Cppo}
; for now think of
T
as the lifting monad. Then a term would be an element of a domain satisfying
D\cong D^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow TD^{\mathbb{N}}
. This domain equation can be solved by standard techniques. Kleisli function composition gives rise to a sequencing operation
D\times D\rightarrow D
which is associative, and forms a monoid with unit element given by the unit of the monad. We will write
(d_{1}.d_{2})
for the composition of two elements of
D
using this operation.

Observe that
D
is a
reflexive object
in
\mathbf{Cppo}
and hence provides a model of the
\lambda
-calculus:

\displaystyle D~\cong~D^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow TD^{\mathbb{N}}~\cong~(D^{\mathbb{N}}\times D)\rightarrow TD^{\mathbb{N}}~\cong~D\rightarrow(D^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow TD^{\mathbb{N}})~\cong~D\rightarrow D.

We briefly spell out the semantics of
\lambda
-calculus induced by this model. Let
\rho
range over
environments
: functions from the set of variables to
D
. We use
s
to range over
D^{\mathbb{N}}
;
(s\cdot d)
denotes the stack resulting from pushing
d
onto
s
. We shall elide the isomorphism
D\cong D^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow TD^{\mathbb{N}}
.
For any term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
and environment
\rho
we define
\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho\in D
(equivalently, a function
D^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow TD^{\mathbb{N}}
) as follows.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x.\,\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho=\rho{x}\qquad\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}[N].\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho~s=\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho~(s\cdot\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho)\qquad\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\langle x\rangle.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho~(s\cdot d)=\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho^{\prime}~s

where
\rho^{\prime}(x)=d
and
\rho^{\prime}(y)=\rho(y)
for
y\not=x
.

These definitions show immediately that application is interpreted by pushing the argument onto the stack, and abstraction by popping a term from the stack. Thus this standard
\lambda
-calculus model directly justifies the machine semantics. It extends to the sequential
\lambda
-calculus by defining

\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho=\eta\quad\quad\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}x.\,M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho=(\rho(x)~.~\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho),

where
\eta
is the unit of the monad (and of the monoid).

Thanks to the compositionality of the semantics we can readily prove:

Lemma 9
.

\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho=\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rho^{\prime}
if
\forall x\in\mathsf{fv}({\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})~\rho(x)=\rho^{\prime}(x).

As a consequence of this Lemma, we may speak of
\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket
, independent of
\rho
, when
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
is closed.

We extend the semantics to stacks of closed terms. Suppose the monad
T
is lifting. A stack
S
denotes an element of
D^{\mathbb{N}}
:

\llbracket\varepsilon\rrbracket=\bot\quad\quad\llbracket S\cdot{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket=\langle\llbracket S\rrbracket,\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\rangle

where we elide the isomorphism
D^{\mathbb{N}}\cong D^{\mathbb{N}}\times D
.

Thanks to the direct correspondence between the semantic equations and the machine transitions, we have:

Lemma 10

(Soundness)
.

Whenever
({S},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\kern 1.0pt{\Downarrow}\kern 1.0pt({T},{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
(with all terms closed),
\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket(\llbracket S\rrbracket)=\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}N\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket(\llbracket T\rrbracket)
.

Theorem 11

(Adequacy)
.

If
\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket(\llbracket S\rrbracket)\neq\bot
then there exists a
T
such that
(S,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\kern 1.0pt{\Downarrow}\kern 1.0pt(T,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
.

Proof.

The proof of this statement follows readily from the existence of three relations: a relation between elements of
D
and closed terms; a relation between semantic streams in
D^{\mathbb{N}}
and streams
S
; and a relation between computations in
TD
and machine states
(S,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})
. We write
\mathbin{\lhd}
for each of these relations, relying on the types to disambiguate. The relations are required to satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle d\mathbin{\lhd}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

iff

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in D^{\mathbb{N}},\sigma\mathbin{\lhd}S\Rightarrow d(\sigma)\mathbin{\lhd}(S,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})

\displaystyle\sigma\mathbin{\lhd}S

iff

\displaystyle\forall i.\sigma_{i}\mathbin{\lhd}S_{i}

\displaystyle k\mathbin{\lhd}(S,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})

iff

\displaystyle k=\mathsf{lift}(\sigma)\Rightarrow(S,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\kern 1.0pt{\Downarrow}\kern 1.0pt(T,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})~\text{and}~\sigma\mathbin{\lhd}T.

These conditions cannot be used as a definition of the relations
\mathbin{\lhd}
, for example as a fixed point of an operator on such relations, because the first clause contains a negatively-occurring usage of
\mathbin{\lhd}
. Nevertheless the existence of such relations can be established using standard techniques of denotational semantics. Pitts’s work
[
11
]
gives an elegant general theory which enables the construction of such relations.

Once
\mathbin{\lhd}
has been shown to exist, a straightforward induction on syntax establishes that for any term
{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and any finite stream
S
, we have

\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket\mathbin{\lhd}{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\quad\llbracket S\rrbracket\mathbin{\lhd}S\quad\quad\llbracket{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rrbracket(\llbracket S\rrbracket)\mathbin{\lhd}(S,{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})

from which computational adequacy immediately follows.
∎

Note that our soundness and adequacy results are expressed in terms of the stack-machine evaluation mechanism. It is also the case that the denotational semantics validates the beta- and eta-laws of Definition
7
, but our point in this section is to emphasise that the stack-machine semantics can be seen as an implementation of a natural denotational model.

Our denotational semantics so far gives an account of the sequential
\lambda
-calculus. To extend to the FMC, we replace the lifting monad with the
state monad

TX=St\rightarrow(St\times X)_{\bot}
, where
St
is a domain of states. Our domain equation becomes

D\cong D^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow(St\rightarrow(St\times D^{\mathbb{N}})_{\bot})\cong St\times D^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow(St\times D^{\mathbb{N}})_{\bot}

If we let
St=D^{\mathbb{N}}
, so that the values in the state are stacks, this is a domain equation for “two-stack transformers”. As above, this is a reflexive object, now in
two distinct ways
depending on which stack is used to interpret the arguments. This is exactly the FMC with two locations; extension to any finite set of locations is handled similarly, and the soundness and adequacy results may be proved in the same way. As we emphasized in the introduction, this semantics has the remarkable property that the stack used to interpret the operations of the
\lambda
-calculus has exactly the same status as that used to interpret state, and it is merely convention that distinguishes the two. This is precisely the point of view embodied by the novel syntax and operational semantics of the FMC.

\displaystyle{\vphantom{(}\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma}}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}~\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}\star\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0.8}\kern 0.4pt\accentset{\leftharpoonup}{\tau\kern 0.4pt}\!{}_{A}{\kern 1.0pt\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}}}{\mathbin{\raise
